tactic complexity (for an extensive discussion, see Heine & Kuteva 2007): 
	
(2)	
Major diachronic trends relevant to language evolution: 
	
	 • concrete words > abstract words
	
	 • abstract lexical words > grammatical morphemes
	
	 • deontic modality > epistemic senses
	
	 • chained/conjoined clauses > complex/embedded clauses
	
	 • pre-grammar (discourse-pragmatic) rules > syntactic (grammatical-
ized) rules
We will see further below how these general trends combine with more specific 
insights from the diachrony of grammar in fleshing out concrete proposals about 
the evolution of grammar.
12.5  Pre-grammatical pidgin and language evolution: The parallels
As suggested earlier above (ch. 9), pidgin communication has, potentially, 
strong bearing on our understanding of language evolution, and within it on 
.  The main factors that lead to the attrition of grammatical morphology is phonological 
erosion, primarily through sound assimilation. The attrition of syntactic constructions is due 
primarily to the attrition and subsequent irregularization of their attendant morphology (Givón 
1979; Dahl 2004).

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
the evolution of syntactic complexity. First, the regularities (‘rules’) of pre-
grammatical communication as seen in both early child language and second 
language pidgins probably characterized pre-grammar as an evolutionary stage. 
Second, pidgin communication is much more context-dependent than fully-
grammaticalized communication. And third, pidgin  communication reveals 
several structural features that suggest an earlier evolutionary stage. These are: 
	
(3)	
Major pidgin structural features relevant to language evolution: 
	
	 • mostly-concrete vocabulary
	
	 • lack of grammatical morphology and well-marked constructions
	
	 • mostly short (1—3-word) clauses
	
	 • clause chaining rather than embedding
	
	 • pragmatic rather than syntactic pre-grammar rules
	
	 • highly context-dependent communication
Given the striking similarity between second language pidgin communication 
and early child language, there is no reason to refrain from considering pre-
grammatical pidgins — whether in children or adults — as a legitimate analog 
to a distinct stage in language evolution.
12.6  The evolution of syntactic complexity
We come now to the core of this chapter, where one must put together coher-
ently the multi-step, gradual evolutionary scenario for the rise of human language, 
grammar, and syntactic complexity.
12.6.1  Communicative ecology
a.  The rise of displaced spatio-temporal reference
Both early childhood and pre-human communication are heavily invested in the 
here-and-now, you-and-I, this-or-that referents that are accessible to all partici-
pants in the shared immediate speech situation. When all referents are equally 
accessible to all participants on the scene, coding the lexical type of referents is 
superfluous. Mere pointing — deixis, directing the interlocutor’s attention to the 
referent — will suffice.
Mature human communication is, in contrast, heavily tilted towards spatio- 
temporally displaced referents, be they agents, objects, states or events. This is 
reflected first in the lopsided use-frequencies of displaced reference. But it is also 
reflected in the fact that so much of our grammatical machinery is dedicated to com-
municating about displaced referents, states and events (Givón 1995, ch. 2; 2001).

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
Referents in the shared immediate speech-situation are mentally represented 
in working memory or focal attention. Such representation shifts — with motion 
and attention — from one moment to the next, and is thus temporally unstable. In 
contrast, displaced referents are more likely to be represented in episodic memory, 
either as recollections of past experience or projections of future plans, wishes, 
imaginations. Compared to working memory, episodic memory is a much more 
time-stable, longer-term mental representation.6
The rise of the human lexical code — phonology — may now be understood 
as an adaptation designed to accommodate the shift from non-displaced to dis-
placed reference. When the adaptively-relevant topics of human communication 
became temporally and spatially displaced — away from the scene, remembered 
past or projected future — mere pointing ceased to be a viable tool of shared refer-
ence. Coding the mental lexicon by more specific means now became an adaptive 
necessity. It is hardly an accident that the most conspicuous lexical-like signals of 
pre-human primates are predator calls, produced in the most adaptively-urgent, 
context — life-and-death.
In the same vein, the shift to displaced referents, and the expansion of the range 
and complexity of the universe of reference, now require an extended structural 
machinery for referent tracking. Establishing shared reference, above and beyond 
the old shared-situation conventions of pointing and joint attention, became a 
pressing issue. The rise of the requisite grammatical machinery — articles, pro-
nouns, grammatical case-markers, restrictive modifiers — is testimony to the new 
adaptive pressures exerted by the expanded universe of reference. Within this 
context, the no-doubt-late genesis of relative clauses to handle the most complex 
problems of shared reference can now be understood. It is adaptive pressures in 
the domain of reference that motivated, as they do in language ontogeny, the rise 
of this major component of syntactic complexity.
b.  The rise of declarative speech-acts
As noted earlier above, spontaneous pre-human communication is confined almost 
exclusively to manipulative speech-acts, a tendency also observed in early child-
hood communication. In contrast, mature human communication is tilted heav-
ily, at the use-frequency level, toward declarative speech-acts (Givón 1995, ch. 2).  
And the bulk of the grammatical machinery of human language is invested in cod-
ing informative speech-acts (Givón 2001).
.  This temporal stability may have contributed toward the objectivization of verbally-coded 
referents, including mental predicates such as ‘think’, ‘know’, ‘see’, ‘understand’, ‘want’, ‘be able’, 
etc. Such predicates are central to the representation of other minds; and, incidentally, they are 
strongly implicated in the genesis of one type of syntactic complexity, verb complements.

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
The emergence of declarative communication also points toward the increasing 
adaptive relevance of displaced referents. Manipulative speech-acts are confined to 
the here-and-now, you-and-I, this-and-that; that is, to the immediate speech situ-
ation. They thus pertain to, primarily, information represented in working memory 
and focal attention. Declarative and interrogative speech-acts, on the other hand, 
are utterly superfluous when all referents are equally accessible to all interlocutors 
here-and-now. Why bother to tell another person about states of affairs s/he surely 
knows? Why bother to ask them if you already know what they know?
It is the emergence of displaced referents as the more prevalent topic of commu-
nication that endowed declarative and interrogative speech-acts with their adaptive 
urgency: They are designed to carry the load of reporting and querying about spatially 
inaccessible referents and past or future events, information that is not available to all 
interlocutors in the immediate speech situation. The rise of displaced reference created 
an informational imbalance in the intimate social unit, and declarative and interroga-
tive speech-acts were the adaptive response designed to remedy the imbalance.
The emergence of declarative speech-acts is, further, part and parcel of the lib-
eration of epistemic predicates from their erstwhile subordination to deontic pred-
icates. This liberation led to separate and more explicit representation of epistemic 
mental states — ‘think’, ‘know’, ‘see’, ‘hear, ‘understand’’ etc. And this, in turn, may 
have contributed to heightened awareness of mental framing operations, first of 
those pertaining to one’s own mind and then, by extension, of those pertaining to 
the minds of others. As the Theory of Mind literature has noted, representing of the 
self’s mind is a pre-condition for representing other minds.7
It is worth noting in this connection that manipulative (deontic) speech-acts 
already contain within them the germ of declarative (epistemic) speech-acts — as 
Gricean presuppositions (Austin 1962; Grice 1968/1975). That is, some background 
knowledge must be there in order for the deontic speech-act to be felicitous. As a 
simple illustration of this, consider:
	
(4)	
a.	
Deontic speech-act: 
	
	
	
I want you to give me the apple.
	
	
b.	 Epistemic presuppositions about the world: 
	
	
	
You have the apple now, I don’t.
	
	
	
In my intended future, I will have the apple, you won’t.
	
	
c.	
Social-deontic presuppositions about the speaker & hearer: 
	
	
	
I have a certain right to request the apple from you.
	
	
	
You have a certain predisposition to comply with my request.
.  See discussion in Givón (2005, ch. 4).

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
The change from deontic to epistemic is thus a case of semantic bleaching, or 
liberation of the epistemic from its erstwhile subservience to the deontic. This 
kind of liberation is, of course, greatly facilitated by displaced temporal reference. 
Direct speech-acts are only felicitous in the here-and-now (Austin 1962). The min-
ute you recast them in the past or future, they lose their illocutionary force. Thus, 
neither (5a) nor (5b) below can be counted as manipulative speech-acts, but only 
as declarative descriptions of past or future manipulative speech-acts (see ch. 7):
	
(5)	
a.	
I wanted you to give me the apple yesterday.
	
	
b.	 I will want you to give me the apple tomorrow.
c.  The rise of multi-propositional discourse
Both early childhood and primate communication are overwhelmingly mono-
propositional. That is, their discourse coherence scope does not extend beyond the 
single proposition, so that the topic of communication shifts from one utterance to 
the next. In contrast, mature human communication is, at the use-frequency level, 
overwhelmingly multi-propositional, with long equi-topic clause chains, thematic 
paragraphs and coherent episodes. This is also reflected in the fact that the bulk of 
the machinery of grammar is invested in coding multi-propositional, cross-clausal 
coherence (Givón 2001). And discourse coherence translates, in cognitive terms, 
into representing the shifting epistemic and deontic states of the interlocutor dur-
ing ongoing communication (ch. 2, above; Givón 2005).
One may view the rise of multi-propositional discourse as but the next 
logical-adaptive step in the rise of declarative communication. As the volume 
of adaptively-relevant information about displaced referents piled up higher, 
faster and more streamlined processing of such information became useful. 
This is especially true in terms of the constant perspective-shifting involved in 
the processing of longer stretches of coherent discourse. The rise of grammar 
may be viewed as an adaptive response to the need to process this rising tide of 
declarative multi-propositional information, as well as to the attendant need to 
construct more efficient mental models of other minds.
d.  Grammar and other minds
As noted earlier (ch. 2), grammar codes, primarily, the speaker’s mental models 
of the hearer’s shifting epistemic and deontic states during ongoing communi-
cation. The high automaticity of grammar may mean, among other things, that 
the evolution of grammatical communication was motivated, at least in part, by 
the strong adaptive pressure of having to process a high frequency of perspective 
shifts during communication (MacWhinney 2002, 2008). The frequency of such 
shifting in human communication may be orders-of-magnitudes higher than in 
pre-human communication.

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
The increased dependence on the representation of other minds may also be 
linked to the greatly increased reliance, in human communication, on a type of 
context that is utterly unique to human communication — episodic memory of 
the current text. This memory capacity is specifically adapted to represent spatio-
temporally displaced reference. The reliance on the interlocutor’s representation of 
specific episodic — rather than generic or situational — knowledge may constitute 
added adaptive pressure for developing a streamlined, automated system of repre-
senting other minds — grammar.
Within grammar, lastly, embedded verb complements arose because mental 
verbs in main clauses — ‘want’, ‘know’, ‘see’, ‘say’ etc. — are used to express the deon-
tic and epistemic modality the speaker assigns to propositions (as well as to mark 
the proposition’s tense-aspect-modal perspective; Givón 2005, ch. 6). And relative 
clauses, as noted earlier above, arose to guide the hearer through the cognitive-
epistemic labyrinth of complex reference. Complex syntax is thus not about some 
abstract structural complexity (‘recursivity’). Rather, it is the predictable structural 
consequence of the adaptive need for complex Theory of Mind operations.
12.6.2  Cultural ecology
What we have seen in the preceding section boils down to a fairly straight- 
forward chain of causation: Somehow, be the reason what it may, it became adap-
tively advantageous for early hominids to start communicating not only about the 
here-and-now, you-and-I, this-and-that visible, the way pre-human primates still 
do, but also about spatially- and temporally-displaced referents; and about states 
and events that were either memory traces of the past or projected traces of an 
imagined or planned future. Most germane, adaptively useful information was not 
anymore shared equally across the hominid social group. Somehow, the prior bal-
ance of informational homogeneity and stability characteristic of pre-human pri-
mate society became disrupted. And in conjunction, knowing the states of belief 
and intention of one’s conspecifics became more adaptively urgent.
There are three obvious contextual factors that could engender such 
change in the erstwhile informational stability and homogeneity of early hom-
inid society: 
a greatly expanded foraging range, particularly for small hunting, scouting or 
• 
exploring groups, bringing back information about adaptive relevance displaced 
referents.
a great increase in the size of the relevant social-communicative group, diluting 
• 
the erstwhile cross-group homogeneity of information.
an accelerated rate of cultural change, and with it a higher level of cultural-
• 
informational specialization within the group.

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
The first factor increases the number of adaptively-relevant displaced refer-
ents, especially when the increased foraging range is confined to relatively small 
groups fanning out in different directions. And all three factors contribute to the 
rise of an informational imbalance within the group, thus creating an adaptive 
pressure for the rise of informative speech-acts, multi-propositional discourse and 
a streamlined system of well-coded communication.
One need not be reductive about the exact causation of the information 
explosion in hominid evolution. Many factors have been mentioned as the initial 
adaptive impetus: sexual selection (Darwin 1871; Miller 2001), descent from the 
trees, moving to the savanna, bipedism, expanding foraging range, omnivorous 
feeding, tool-making (Greenfield 1991), social grooming (Dunbar 1992), group 
identity (Knight 1998), increased group size (Dunbar 1992), big-game hunting 
(Washburn & Lancaster 1968), big-game scavenging (Bickerton 2005), pre-hunt 
consultation (Számadó 2008), laryngeal retraction (Lieberman 1984) or theory 
of mind (Dunbar 1998; Tomasello et al. 2005).
A more realistic scenario would, I believe, be complex, multi-variant and co-
evolutionary, along the less reductive lines of de Waal (2001); Geary (2005); Szá-
madó & Szathmáry (2006) or Cheney & Seyfarth (2007). It may well be that many, 
perhaps most, of these factors are correlated. But whatever was the earliest cause, it 
must account for the profound disruption of the informational stability and homo-
geneity in the early-hominid society of intimates. And it must explain why specific 
knowledge by conspecifics became both inaccessible and adaptively vital to group 
members, to the point where a better-coded communication system became an 
adaptive must.
12.6.3  The rise of well-coded communication
In this section I will summarize the major evolutionary steps that must have 
taken place in the rise of the two major communicative codes of human language, 
lexical phonology and grammar. The sequence of steps given below constitutes a 
hypothesis, but one that is relatively easy to come by, given our previous survey 
of the facts of animal communication, language ontogeny, language diachrony 
and pidginization.
12.6.3.1  The rise of the well-coded lexicon
As noted above, as long as the referents of communication were equally accessible 
to all interlocutors in the immediate communicative situation, and thus as long 
as the topics of communication were concrete, symbolic-phonological coding of 
nominal referents (objects) was unnecessary. Pointing to attract the interlocutor’s 
attention would have done the job of well-coded lexical nouns.

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
Further, as long as cultural stability, homogeneity and simplicity prevailed, 
the association of objects or animate beings with the stereotypical actions/states/
events normally pertaining to them was predictable on cultural-generic grounds. 
What else does one do with foodstuff but eat? What else does one do with a new-
born child except feed and care? What else does one do with an unrelated same-
age member of the opposite sex except (potentially) mate? What else does one do 
with a shelter except hide? What else does one do with a round rock except throw 
at an adversary, or at a game animal, or crack a nut? What else does one use a 
sharp object for except to cut? What else does one do with a game animal except 
hunt? What else does one do with grains and grubs and roots and berries except 
gather and eat? What else does one do with strangers except fight or run? Given 
the cultural milieu of the pre-human society of intimates, neither symbolically-
coded nouns nor verbs were a high communicative priority.
The rise of the well-coded lexicon, thus of phonology, is the direct result of 
both displaced reference and the disruption of cultural/informational stability 
and homogeneity. To judge by current non-human primate communication, 
both gestural-visual and auditory-oral coding could have arisen gradually and 
in tandem. The availability of the ventral visual object-recognition channel in 
the primate brain suggests that perhaps it may have been adaptively advanta-
geous to place a heavier coding-load, at least initially, on the visual-gestural 
channel, thus taking advantage of the inherent iconicity of visual representa-
tion (Kaas 1989; Givón 1995, ch. 9). The extreme ease, naturalness and sponta-
neity with which non-hearing human children develop a gestural-visual code 
(Goldin-Meadow 2002; Morford 2002) suggests that the requisite neurology is 
still there; and there is no reason to assume it wasn’t just as available to early 
hominids. The essentially-similar object or word, and event or clause, process-
ing times for visually- and verbally-presented information suggests the same 
(Barker & Givón 2002). And gestural-visual coding remains a parallel, available 
channel alongside the oral-auditory in current human communication.
There are also equally good adaptive reasons why an eventual switch, at least in 
terms of relative coding load, to auditory-oral coding became an adaptive advan-
tage later on (Givón 1995, ch. 9): 
communication about abstract referents
• 
increased vocabulary size
• 
communication over visual barriers or in the dark
• 
carrying distance of the signal
• 
One may as well note, lastly, that recent neurological work suggests the pres-
ence in pre-human primates of a brain module dedicated to vocal communication 
among conspecifics. Thus Poremba et al. (2004) report that in the left superior 

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
temporal gyrus of rhesus monkeys, where the primary auditory center is located, 
the temporal pole is specifically activated only by conspecifics’ vocalization, but not 
by any other sounds. This suggests that pre-human primates already have a spe-
cialized sub-module in the primary auditory left-cortex dedicated to auditory-oral 
communication. An earlier report by Poremba et al. (2003) also suggests that the 
primary auditory cortex is a vast network, comparable to the visual system, span-
ning over the left superior temporal gyrus as well as parietal, pre-frontal and limbic 
sites. It thus appears more and more likely that the early hominid lexical-semantic 
code may have started as both gestural-visual and auditory-oral.
12.6.3.2  Early pre-grammatical pidgin: The one-word clause
The rise of early pre-grammatical pidgin, the one-word clause, was no doubt 
strongly associated with the shift to declarative speech-acts. One-word clauses, 
mostly nouns to begin with as in the corresponding stage of early child language 
(Bloom 1973; Scollon 1976), could now function as referring declarative expres-
sions, not only as manipulative speech-acts. As in early childhood, the exact 
specification of the state/event/action in this type of early-pidgin communication 
was most likely negotiated across adjacent participant turns. Coding the verbs 
remained a lower priority — as long as the speech-situation and cultural stability 
and homogeneity allowed the verb to be inferred. And as noted in chapter 10, the 
immediate context could easily supplement the requisite missing verb — as long as 
communication remained highly dependent on culturally-shared knowledge and 
the immediate speech situation.
12.6.3.3  Late pre-grammatical pidgin: Two-word clauses and  
multi-propositional discourse
As noted earlier above, for as long as communication was about concrete entities 
that partook in concrete states/events, and for as long as the culture remained  
stable, predictable, homogeneous and compact, coding verbs was not a high adap-
tive priority. But with multi-propositional discourse, displaced reference, a disrup-
tion of early cultural homogeneity and an increased number and diversity of the 
topics of communication, the lexicalization of verbs became adaptively relevant.
As in child language, two-word clauses in language evolution must have  
been comprised of two nouns earlier on, and only gradually switched to noun-
verb combinations. Indeed, as noted in ch. 10, verbless clauses are still used exten-
sively in human communication, and are an extremely well-governed grammatical 
construction. The greater communicative efficacy of noun-noun combinations, 
be they agent-patient, patient-recipient, patient-instrument, recipient-location, 

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
patient-location or agent-location, is due to the fact that it greatly constrains 
the choice of the appropriate lexical verb, as compared to the one-noun clause.  
Noun-noun clauses may have thus served, as in child language acquisition, as early 
precursors of noun-verb or verb-noun clauses.
Once the message became multi-propositional, thus chained two-word-clause 
discourse, and once the assumptions that the speaker must make about the hear-
er’s mental states during ongoing communication aggregated, the adaptive pres-
sure for the evolution of grammar was on. It is unlikely that the response was 
instantaneous. Rather, as in language ontogeny and diachrony, the evolution of 
morpho-syntax must have proceeded gradually, in multiple steps. And it may have 
spanned scores of thousands or hundreds of thousands of years. In the following 
sections I will outline some of the more likely major steps.
With the advent of multi-word clauses, early hominids have arrived at level-I 
of syntactic complexity, where an abstract clause-node governs a cluster of word- 
nodes (ch. 10). When noun modifiers appear together with their head nouns 
(noun phrase), or when objects appear together with their verbs (verb phrase), the 
simple clause is already a hierarchic structure.
12.6.3.4  The rise of pre-grammar
As noted earlier above (ch. 2,9,10), the conventions of early-childhood and  
second-language pre-grammatical pidgin, and thus by implication of the correspond-
ing language-evolution stage, are highly iconic and cognitively transparent. Most 
likely, this early-stage grammar applied to clauses that were one-word, two-word or 
at most three-word long. This assumption is warranted by the observation that to this 
day, in natural spoken discourse, clause length seldom exceeds 2–3 words (Givón 
1975; DuBois 1987; Chafe 1994). At the pre-grammar stage, further, both early child-
hood and adult second-language pidgin communication are devoid of grammatical 
morphology and well-marked syntactic constructions. The rules of pre-grammar 
must have thus applied to clauses that grouped together nouns, adjectives and verbs.
The rules of pre-grammar (ch. 9) are reproduced below with slight modifica-
tions. Consider first intonation and rhythm rules, the subliminal music of language: 
	
(6)	
Intonation and rhythm rules: 
	
	
a.	
Stress and predictability: 
	
	
	
“Less-predictable information chunks are stressed”.
	
	
b.	 Stress and importance: 
	
	
	
“More important information chunks are stressed”.
	
	
c.	
Melodic contour and relevance: 
	
	
	
“Information chunks that belong together conceptually are  
packed together under a unified melodic contour”.

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
	
	
d.	 Pause and rhythm: 
	
	
	
“The size of the temporal break between information chunks  
corresponds to the size of the cognitive or thematic distance  
between them”.
Rules (6a,6b) are still extant in the use of emphatic stress by itself, as in  
English. They are also incorporated into more grammaticalized constructions, such 
as stressed independent pronouns, stressed demonstratives, cleft, Y-movement  
or WH-questions. Rules (6c,d) pertain to the bracketing together of clause-size 
chunks of communication, i.e. intonational packaging (Goldman-Eisler 1968; 
Chafe 1994; Mithun 2006, 2007a, 2007b, 2008; Givón 1991a).
Consider next the rules that govern the spacing of constituents, in the sense of 
proximity (vs. distance) of the sub-units off the phrase or clause to each other: 
	
(7)	
Spacing rules: 
	
	
a.	
Proximity and relevance: 
	
	
	
“Information chunks that belong together conceptually are  
kept in closer spatio-temporal proximity”.
	
	
b.	 Proximity and scope: 
	
	
	
“Functional operators are kept closest to the operand to which  
they are relevant”.
Rules (8a,b) go the heart of packaging words together into multi-level 
hierarchic units, such as phrases or clauses. They predict a preference for non-
scattered constituents, for putting operators next to operands and modifiers 
next to modified, and in general for placing the words belonging to the same 
phrase next to each other, and the phrases belonging to the same clause next to 
each other.
Consider next the rules of pre-grammar that govern pragmatic — as distinct 
from syntactic — word order: 
	
(8)	
Sequential precedence rules: 
	
	
a.	
Order and importance: 
	
	
	
“A more important information chunk is fronted”.
	
	
b.	 Occurrence order and reported order: 
	
	
	
“The temporal order in which events occurred will be mirrored  
in the language-coded report of the events”.
In languages with flexible, pragmatically-governed word-order, rule (8a) gov-
erns the placement of important topics in the clause-initial position, including  
referring-indefinite NPs. In grammaticalized syntactic constructions, this rule 
predicts the fronting of WH-question words, the fronting of the focused topical 

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
element in cleft and Y-movement constructions, and the fronting of the topical-
ized constituent in L-dislocation (Givón 1988a, 2001).
Consider, lastly, the rules of pre-grammar that govern the phonological size of 
words and morphemes: 
	
(9)	
Quantity rules: 
	
	
a.	
Zero expression and predictability: 
	
	
	
“Predictable—or already activated—information will be left  
unexpressed”.
	
	
b.	 Zero expression and relevance: 
	
	
	
“Unimportant or irrelevant information will be left unexpressed”.
Rule (9a) predicts the use of zero-anaphora for continuing referents (Givón ed. 
1983a, 1992) and continuing predicates (ch. 10). It also predicts the size difference 
between independent pronouns (stressed, contrastive) and anaphoric pronouns 
(unstressed, clitic). Rule (9b) predicts zero expression of the agent of passives and 
the patient of the antipassive.
In grammaticalized communication, the rules of pre-grammar are integrated 
with the more arbitrary (‘symbolic’) devices of grammar — morphology, complex-
hierarchic constructions, grammatical word-order, scope and government con-
ventions, etc. (Givón 1989, ch. 3)
12.6.3.5  The rise of grammatical morphology
There is a vast literature on the rise of grammatical morphemes out of lexical 
words.8 What we need to note here is that grammatical morphemes do not arise 
just from words, but from words in specific syntactic constructions, be they noun 
phrases, verb phrases or concatenated clauses. What grammaticalization gives rise 
to, then, are morphologically-marked syntactic constructions. And the construc-
tion within which a morpheme arises, together with the semantics of the precursor 
word, determine the kind of morpheme it will be, and whether it will be a noun 
(-phrase) clitic, verb (-phrase) clitic, or clause-level clitic. This is the framework 
within which the genesis of complex clauses, such as V-complements and REL-
clauses, should be understood.
Syntactic constructions most commonly include specific grammatical mor-
phemes that mark them, in conjunction with word-order and intonation. In 
languages such as English or Chinese, with relatively scant morphology, this is 
not always apparent. But in most languages, the functional load carried by the 
morphology, particularly verbal morphology, is much higher.
.  For relatively recent summaries, see Givón (2001, vol. 1) and Heine & Kuteva (2007).

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
In this section I will discuss briefly the rise of the two most common, and 
most elaborate, morphological systems: (a) referent-tracking morphology in the 
noun-phrase; and (b) verbal morphology that pertains to tense-aspect-modality, 
voice and transitivity. The important thing to remember is that once words have 
been grouped into phrases and clauses in pre-grammatical pidgin, the stage is set 
for grammaticalization. That is, for the rise of both grammatical morphemes and 
well-marked syntactic constructions — in tandem.
a.  Referent-marking nominal morphology
As noted earlier, the most common referential signal in pre-human communica-
tion is a gestural or vocal deictic signal — pointing, most commonly with the 
head and eyes, aiming to attract the interlocutor’s attention to a referent, i.e., to 
establish joint attention. This deictic gesture most likely gave rise first to a lexical-
ized demonstrative pronoun signal — ‘this’/‘that’. From demonstrative pronouns, 
grammaticalization studies suggest the development of two other, well known 
reference-tracking devices — pronouns and articles:
	 (10)	
a.	
From demonstrative to pronoun: 
	
	
	
DEM > stressed PRO > unstressed (anaphoric, clitic) PRO: 
	
	
	
thát (one) > s/hé > s/he-s-gone
	
	
b.	 From demonstrative to definite article: 
	
	
	
DEM > DEM, N > DEM N > article + N: 
	
	
	
thát > thát, woman > thát woman > the-woman
	
	
c.	
From ‘one’ or ‘this’ to indefinite article: 
	
	
	
Stressed NUM/DEM > important new ref. > new ref.: 
	
	
	
óne man > one-N > a-man
	
	
	
thís guy > this-guy
What (10b) suggests is that nouns were added to the initial deictic pointer as an 
afterthought in a paratactic NP construction that only later became a syntactic 
NP construction.
Subject (nominative) and direct-object (accusative) case-markers are central in 
the grammar of referential coherence, indicating the relative topicality (importance) 
of event participants. However, most commonly these ‘pragmatic’ case-markers are 
not grammaticalized directly from lexical words. Rather, they are derived indirectly 
from previously-grammaticalized semantic case-markers.9
.  The semantic patient or dative marker may be re-grammaticalized into the pragmatic 
direct-object marker. Agent-of-passive, ergative-agent, genitive or dative-subject markers may 
be re-grammaticalized as subject case-markers.

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
Semantic case-markers are grammaticalized directly from lexical words 
through two main channels, de-verbal and de-nominal. The de-verbal channel 
can give rise to a large set of case-roles, via a process that presupposes two chained 
clauses as the precursor (see ch. 4): 
	 (11)	
The serial-verb channel of semantic case-markers: 
	
	
a.	
‘take’ > patient: ‘she took apple, ate’ > ‘she ate the apple’
	
	
b.	
‘take’ > instrument: ‘she took knife, cut meat’ > ‘she with-knife cut meat’
	
	
c.	
‘go’ > allative: ‘she took child, go house’ > ‘she took child to-house’
	
	
d.	 ‘leave’ > ablative: ‘she took child, leave house’ > ‘she took child 
from-house’
	
	
e.	
‘give’ > benefactive: ‘she built house, give man’ > ‘she built house 
for-man’
The de-nominal channel gives rise to, predominantly, various positional loca-
tive case-markers, a process that presupposes a noun phrase as precursor, with one 
noun modifying the other (Givón 2001, vol. I). And it is the erstwhile head noun 
that becomes the grammaticalized case-marker: 
	 (12)	
The relational-noun channel of locative case-markers: 
	
	
a.	
‘face’ > ‘in front’: ‘face (of the) house’ > ‘(in) front (of) the house’
	
	
b.	 ‘back’ > ‘behind’: ‘back (of the) house’ > ‘behind the house’
	
	
c.	
‘head’ > ‘on/above/up’: ‘head (of the) house’ > ‘on top (of) the house’
	
	
d.	 ‘foot’ > ‘under/down’: ‘foot (of the) tree’ > ‘under the tree’
	
	
e.	
‘hand’ > ‘side/near’: ‘hand (of the) man’ > ‘near the man’
Noun-noun compounds in the noun phrase also give rise to nominal classi-
fiers, and again it is the erstwhile head noun that becomes the grammaticalized 
classifier, as in ‘swords-man’ (*‘man of sword’), ‘helms-man’ (*‘man of helm’), 
‘crafts-man’ (*‘man of craft’), etc.10 Modifying nouns may also serve, together 
with restrictive modifying adjectives, as an analogical template for the subse-
quent development of clause-size restrictive modifiers (REL-clauses; see ch. 8).
b.   Verbal morphology
As noted earlier (ch. 4,7), verbal morphology, with the obvious exception of clitic 
anaphoric pronouns (10a), most commonly arises out of two-clause configura-
tions, where the main-clause verb is grammaticalized into a marker of either 
tense-aspect, epistemic or deontic modality, or voice/transitivity marker on the 
complement verb. Some of the more common trends here in are: 
.  When noun classifiers are combined with demonstratives, they can give rise to large series 
of gendered/classed 3rd-person pronouns.

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
	 (13)	
a.	
‘be/stay/stand’ > progressive aspect > present tense: 
	
	
	
‘He is at sitting’ > ‘He-s sitting’
	
	
b.	 ‘have/come/finish’ > perfect aspect > past tense: 
	
	
	
‘She has it, it’s made’ > ‘She has it made’ > She-s-made it’
	
	
c.	
‘want/go’ (request/promise) > irrealis modality > future tense: 
	
	
	
‘I will (it), to leave’ > ‘I’ll leave’
	
	
	
‘I’m going home, to do it’ > ‘I gonna do it’
	
	
d.	 ‘know/think/guess’ > epistemic modality: 
	
	
	
‘You think, she did it?’ > ‘Think she did it?’
	
	
e.	
‘say/hear/see’ > evidential modality: 
	
	
	
‘They say, she left’ > ‘Say she left’
	
	
f.	
‘suffer’ > passive marker: 
	
	
	
‘He suffered, someone fired him’ > ‘He suffered-(being) fired’
	
	
g.	 ‘make/let/get’ > causative marker: 
	
	
	
‘she got him, he left’ > ‘She got him to leave’
As one can see, some of the most elaborate verbal morphology sub-systems can 
arise through this de-verbal grammaticalization channel.
12.6.3.6  The rise of complex/embedded constructions
As can be seen from the discussion directly above (also ch. 4,7), syntactic complexity 
in the verb phrase — the embedding of one clause into the VP of another — arises 
when one verbal clause is recruited to perform a rather specific communicative 
function on another. Those functions, i.e. the adaptive motivation for VP-embed-
ding, are extremely heterogenous (13a—g); and the ensuing syntactic development 
often gives rise to clause union and thus to grammaticalization of one of the two 
predicates, which then cliticizes to the other. The pre-condition for such develop-
ment, one functional, the other structural, may be summarized as follows: 
	 (14)	
Preconditions for the genesis of complex verb phrases: 
	
	
a. Functional evolution: the rise of more abstract senses of the relevant 
predicates (verbs); the use of such abstract senses to modulate and 
enrich the meaning of propositions; first in direct speech-acts, then in 
attribution to third person in displaced-time contexts.
	
	
b.	 Syntactic evolution: 
	
	
	
• prior rise of multi-propositional discourse (clause chains)
	
	
	
•	 prior use of mental predicates with nominal objects (as an ana-
logical template)

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
Preconditions (14a) are the semantic precondition for grammaticalization. Precon-
ditions (14b) are the syntactic precondition for embedding. Syntactic complexity, 
here the embedding of one clause into the verb phrase of another, is but a predict-
able by-product of these two independent developments.
The second major type of embedded clauses — restrictive REL-clause modi-
fying a head noun in the NP — arose as the culmination of the grammar of ref-
erential coherence, as a device to handle three major types of complex reference 
(ch. 5,8). In the process, a conjoined/chained clause was recruited, and treated as 
background or presupposed information: 
	 (15)	
Major complex-reference functions of REL-clauses: 
	
	
a.	
Supplying relevance-anchor to a new topical referent (REF-indefinite): 
	
	
	
‘we saw this man, he was standing near bar’ >
	
	
	
	
‘we saw this man [standing near the bar]’
	
	
b.	 Differentiation between contrasting similar referents: 
	
	
	
‘cut that tree, it has dry leaves’>
	
	
	
	
‘cut the tree [that has dry leaves]’
	
	
c.	
Coming back to a referent after a temporal gap in discourse (definite): 
	
	
	
‘finally the man got up, he was standing near the bar’ >
	
	
	
	
‘finally the man [who was standing near the bar] got up’
The genesis of REL-clauses does not typically lead to clause union, though it 
often leads to the rise of specific subordinator or pronominal morphology (ch. 5; 
Heine & Kuteva 2007). Again, there are two pre-conditions for this development, 
both of them aspects of the rise of coherent multi-propositional discourse; one 
functional, the other structural: 
	 (16)	
Preconditions for the genesis of complex noun phrases: 
	
	
a.	
Functional: Deeming the information in the two clauses mutually 
relevant in three possible ways (15): 
	
	
	
• one clause makes a referent in the other more salient (15a).
	
	
	
• one clause differentiates a referent in the other clause from a 
similar referent (15b).
	
	
	
• one clause can recall a referent in the other clause from episodic 
memory (15c).
	
	
b.	 Structural: 
	
	
	
•  proximity of the two clauses in a clause-chain.
	
	
	
•  existence of word-size restrictive modifiers as analogical templates.

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
Once again syntactic complexity, in this case the embedding of one clause 
into the noun-phrase of another, arises as the by-product of other, adaptively-
motivated developments rooted in the representation of other minds during 
communication.
clause-size restrictive modifiers embedded in the NP, most typically REL-
clauses, may have had an analogical syntactic antecedent — restrictive one-word 
modifiers, such as adjectives or nouns. The development of restrictive REL-clauses 
may have thus followed the progression (schematically): 
	 (17)	
a.	
‘the big house’ > ‘the destroyed house’ > ‘the house that was destroyed’
	
	
b.	 ‘the log house’ > ‘the cooking house’ > ‘the house where one cooks’
This is not to suggest that a REL-clause is derived from a noun or adjective by 
expansion. Rather, word-size restrictive modifiers supplied the analogical tem-
plate that must have preceded the use of clause-size embedded modifiers. One 
could thus make a diachronic-typological prediction: 
	 (18)	
“No language will have embedded restrictive REL-clauses unless it also 
has restrictive modifying adjectives and/or nouns”.
As was noted in ch. 8, the acquisition by children of ‘true’ REL-clauses was pre-
ceded by the acquisition of verbless restrictive modifiers.
12.7  Neuro-cognitive evolution
12.7.1  Distributive multi-modular network of grammar
Grammar is the last to evolve and the most complex component of human com-
munication. Its genesis has depended on multiple lower-level pre-adaptations. 
It is thus rather unlikely that grammar is subserved in the brain by a single neu-
rological module, or coded by a single “grammar gene”. Rather, grammar is sup-
ported in the brain by a vast distributed network that links, in various circuits, 
multiple pre-existing modules (Bookheimer 2002; Kaan 2008; Hagoort 2008). 
Those modules may have arisen initially for adaptive functions that had little to 
do with grammar or language, but were nonetheless amenable to partaking in the 
processing of this new complex mega-function.
The recruitment of pre-existing modules into a distributive network to per-
form new, complex functions has ample precedent in other limbic-cortical net-
works. The executive attention system is such a distributive network (Schneider 
& Chein 2003; Posner & Fan 2004). The visual information processing system is 
such a network (Ungerleider & Mishkin 1982; Kaas 1989; Mesulam 2000; Tucker 

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
2008). The auditory processing system is such a network (Poremba et al. 2003, 
2004). Semantic memory (lexicon) is such a network (Martin & Chao 2001; 
Bookheimer 2002; Badre & Wagner 2007). Given that most of these complex- 
distributive networks are pre-linguistic, and have been only later pressed into the 
service of language processing, the assembly of the distributive networks and sub-
circuits of grammar from pre-existing modules — and sub-circuits — is natural.
The main neuro-cognitive modules that partake in the processing of grammar, 
reviewed earlier (ch. 11), are recapitulated below.11
	
(19)	
Distributive brain-network for grammar: 
	
	
	
Cognitive	
Linguistic	
Neurological
	
	
a.	 semantic memory	
words	
BA 47; ventral network
	
	
b.	 event representation	 simple-clause syntax	
BA44, anterior-STG/STS,
	
	
	
	
	
hippocampus
	
	
c.	 episodic memory	
chained clauses	
hippocampus
	
	
d.	 working memory	
syntactic buffer	
posterior-parietal cortex,
	
	
	
	
	
pre-frontal cortex
	
	
e.	 ToM representation	 communicative functions	 ???
	
	
f.	 executive attention	
relevance/topicality;	
executive attention network
	
	
	
	
perspective shifting
	
	
g.	 complex-hierarchies	 complex-clause syntax	
BA45, posterior-STG/STS
	
	
h.	 morphology	
grammatical morphology	 ???
	
	
i.	 timing module	
temporal coordination	
cerebellum (?)
As many have noted, most of these modules have their neuro-cognitive  
homologs in the pre-human primate brain (Friederici 2008; Kaan 2008; 
Hagoort 2008; Schmahmann, Pandya et al. 2007). They came on line at dif-
ferent evolutionary times, often adapted into their immediate pre-linguistic 
functions from older functions. Their older layers are intertwined with the pro-
jection of the cortex out of the limbic-thalamic mid-brain of pre-mammalians  
(Mesulam 2000). The genesis of grammar is the most recent elabora-
tion of an old and perhaps still unfolding evolutionary story. In this 
vein, Bookheimer (2002) makes the following observation about lexical- 
semantic representation: 
.  The study of the neurology of grammar has reached an explosive stage, where new and 
more specialized sub-modules of the network are announced daily. For the purpose of the dis-
cussion here, it would suffice to note that grammar is represented in the brain by a number of 
multi-modular distributive circuits (Friederici 2008), and that its lower-level modules may still 
perform their pre-linguistic functions (Kaan 2008; Hagoort 2008).

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
“… One general principle appears to emerge consistently regardless of 
which category is under study … the brain regions identified do not appear 
randomly distributed, but rather spatially proximal to brain regions with 
strong sensory or motor association with the conceptual category …”  (2002: 
p. 173; emphases added).
This is a fair rendition of the principle of terminal modification.
12.7.2  Neuro-adaptive plausibility of the evolution of grammar
Grammar sits at the very apex of an incredibly efficient, highly automated 
human communication system. It is a rapid processor for encoding the inter-
locutor’s shifting deontic and epistemic states during communication. For the 
evolution of grammar to be neurologically plausible, it must be understood as an 
extension of well established and readily available pre-linguistic precursors.
The core adaptive function of grammar, the representation of other minds 
during communication, is by far the least understood aspect of its neuro-evolu-
tion. The following is thus, of necessity, a speculative attempt to articulate what 
must be, eventually, accounted for.
a.  Conscious intentional organisms have an automatic co-activation relation 
between their motor control system and their intention activation system. This is 
a necessary correlate of the organism’s capacity for intentional motion and action.
b.  The work on the pre-motor mirror neuron system (Rizzolatti & Gentilucci 
1988; Rizzolatti et al. 1996a, 1996b, 2000; Milner & Goodale 1995; Goodale 2000) 
suggests the existence of an automatic co-activation relation between the motor 
and visual systems, in both the pre-motor cortex and the dorsal visual trend.
c.  In particular, the mirror-neuron literature suggests that viewing the actions of 
a conspecific automatically activates one’s own motor system. Given (a) above, the 
co-activation of the visual and motor systems thus implies the co-activation of the 
intentional system. Put another way, representations of one’s own mind can be 
activated by the motor and visual systems.
d.  There are strong indications that monkeys, let alone chimpanzees and bono-
bos, already view their conspecifics as intentional beings (Cheney & Seyfarth 
2007); that is, as beings whose actions are activated by their intentions.12
.  In contrast, the representation in the pre-human primate mind of conspecific 
epistemic/belief states is not demonstrated in monkeys (Cheyney & Seyfarth 2007), and 
lags behind deontic/intentional representation in apes (Tomasello et al. 2005). The same 
temporal lag of the epistemic behind the deontic is also seen in children (Wellman 1990; 
Diessel 2005; see ch. 7,8).

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
e.  All this taken together suggests a natural mechanism whereby viewing the 
interlocutor’s actions will also activate a mental model, in the viewer’s mind, of the 
interlocutor’s intentions.
f.   If it is true that the evolution of language piggy-backed on the pre-existing 
visual information processing streams and its ­limbic-anchored semantic and 
episodic memory systems (Givón 1995, ch. 9), as well as on the pre-existing 
motor system (Greenfield 1991), then grammar as an instrument of rapid 
encoding of the interlocutor’s intentional states during communication now has 
a plausible evolutionary pathway: the three-way co-activation relation between 
the visual, motor and intentional systems.
g.  The facts of evolutionary neurology (Mesulam 2000), language development 
(ch. 6,7,8), the diachrony of grammar (chs 3,4,5) and primatology (Premack & 
Woodruff 1978; de Waal 2001; Cheney & Seyfarth 2007) suggest that the mental 
representation of epistemic modality (belief) during communication was initially 
embedded in the mental representation of deontic modality (intention). Only 
later on does epistemic modality liberate itself from the deontic.
12.8  Closure: The genesis of syntactic complexity
The human communication system seems to be, on the face of it, a straight-for-
ward combinatorial affair. words are combined into clauses. clauses are com-
bined into multi-clause chains of coherent discourse. And coherent two-clause 
chains are condensed into complex-embedded clauses. One is thus tempted, 
like a good engineer, to propose the obvious: that the genesis of syntactic 
complexity progressed in an orderly combinatorial fashion, beginning with 
the atomic units of meaning (words), which are then combined into clauses, 
which are then combined into clause-chains; which can then be condensed into 
complex-embedded constructions. This certainly echoes the combinatorial sys-
tem suggested by Tallerman (2007) or Bickerton (2008). The problem with this 
engineering approach to the genesis of syntactic complexity is that it does not 
capture the way the process happens in language ontogeny and language dia-
chrony, or could have plausibly happened in language evolution.
The first thing to remember is that Homo sapiens did not assemble its com-
munication system from scratch. Rather, s/he built it on the pre-existing pri-
mate neuro-cognitive platform, a complex representational system that included 
semantic memory (words), event representation (clauses) and coherent multi-
event episodic representation (clause-chains). The adaptive impetus that led Homo 
sapiens to go beyond this pre-human platform and launch a communication 
system was not rooted in the adaptive demands of representation, but rather of  

	
Chapter 12.  Syntactic complexity and language evolution	 
communication; that is, in the need to transfer rapidly non-obvious, unshared 
information from one mind to another.
Second, the primate ventral and dorsal trends of visual information  
processing — object recognition (lexicon) and event/state recognition (proposi-
tions and multi-propositions), respectively — came to us already fully coupled. 
Objects were recognized as participants in some event/state. And events were 
recognized as having particular types of participants. The rich cross-connectivity 
between the two neural trends (Kaas 1989) suggests that one is never activated 
without the other. Events/states (dorsal trend) provided the context within which 
objects are perceived or construed as adaptively meaningful. The pre-human per-
ception of objects — fauna, flora, inanimates — as adaptively relevant has always 
been couched in the context of some event or state: Where are they? What do 
they do? How do they interact with each other? And most relevant, how do they 
interact with us? What do we do with them? In the same vein, states and events 
are perceived or construed in their adaptively-relevant context, that of coherent 
scenarios that are relevant to us.
The third thing to remember is that well-coded words, although clearly the 
first step in the development of human communication and thus of syntactic 
complexity, were never used just to code concepts. This is because lexical words, 
while having meaning, by themselves carry no message. They are only useful in 
communication if they code propositional messages.
The first step in the genesis of syntactic complexity (see ch. 10) was not just 
taking words with lexical meanings and combining them into clauses with event/
state meaning. Rather, it was taking single words that already coded event/state 
meanings and — relying on the immediate context — coding the other word(s) to 
yield a multi-word verbal clause. This is how adult speakers, be they native, pid-
gin speakers or Broca’s aphasia patients, produce and interpret one-word clauses 
to this day, as a matter of course and in an amazingly well-governed fashion. And 
this is clearly how children still proceed from one-word verbless clauses to multi-
word verbal clauses.
The upshot of all this is that an already complex pre-linguistic represen-
tational system gave rise, in stages, to a complex communication system. That 
is, just as pre-existing event/state representation was the cognitive launching 
pad for multi-word verbal clauses, so was episodic multi-event representation 
the launching pad for clause-chains; and so was complex episodic coherence 
the launching pad for the condensation of chained clauses into complex-
embedded clauses.
The evolutionary sequence I have proposed here involves, at each stage, the 
recruitment of pre-existing, pre-linguistic brain modules to perform related-
but-not-identical novel functions. And the process as a whole was driven by the 

	 The Genesis of Syntactic Complexity
gradually-escalating adaptive pressures that motivated the rise of well-coded 
human communication.
The rise of embedded-recursive syntax is neither a puzzle of non-Darwinian 
evolution, as Hauser et al. (2002) would have it, nor in itself a direct adaptive- 
selected goal. Rather, it is the structural by-product of the adaptive pressures that 
motivated the genesis of grammar. Complex syntactic structure is but the global 
consequence of more mundane, local, adaptively motivated steps. In this way, the 
evolution of morpho-syntactic complexity is the story of how a complex mental 
representation launched a complex communicative behavior under specific adap-
tive pressures. It is also the story of assembling pre-existing neural processors into 
new and more complex circuitry in support of emergent new functions.
Within this overall story, the rise of syntaxis out of parataxis is but the last 
step in the gradual accretion of structural complexity. It is not primarily a shift 
from one structure (coordination) to another (subordination). Rather, it is a shift 
from performing communicative functions with relatively little dedicated struc-
ture, to performing them with explicitly dedicated structures. That is, a shift from 
relying on slow, error-prone attention-demanding contextual inferences to relying 
on more automated, rule-governed processing. Above all, it is the story of how 
meaningful adaptive information was gradually transferred from the surrounding 
context to the dedicated code.
